A solution of 2D incompressible Euler equation with algebraic spiral roll-up in the presence of Wiener type perturbation
Woohyu Jeon

Abstract
. Extending the results of Elling [ 12 , 13 ] , we construct a weak solution of 2D incompressible Euler equation with initial vorticity of the form w 0 ( x ) = | x | − 1 / μ g ( θ ) w_{0}(x)={\left|x\right|}^{-1/\mu}g(\theta) , where g ∈ L p ​ ( 𝕋 ) g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}) satisfies ∑ ℤ | n | − 0.5 ​ | g ^ ​ ( n ) | < ∞ \sum_{\mathbb{Z}}{\left|n\right|}^{-0.5}{\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|}<\infty . In particular, the solution is self-similar and shows algebraic spiral roll-up.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q31, 35Q35, 76B03

1.
Introduction

1.1.
History

One of the most interesting phenomenon which has captured our interest is the flow of non-viscous fluid, e.g., ideal gas. In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, its dynamics can be modeled through the well-known incompressible Euler equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} &u_{t}+\nabla_{x}\cdot(u\otimes u)+\nabla_{x}p=0,\\
&\nabla_{x}\cdot u=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(EE)

or through its vorticity form

\left\{\begin{aligned} &w_{t}+\nabla_{x}\cdot(wu)=0,\\
&u=\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi,\quad w=\Delta_{x}\psi.\end{aligned}\right.

(EE-v)

Here,
\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}),\nabla_{x}^{\perp}=(-\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{1}})
1
1

1

We use subscript below nabla to clarify which coordinate system we are considering.
and
u:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
denotes a velocity field,
p:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes a pressure,
w:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a scalar vorticity, and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a stream function.

Due to its importance, a various aspect of the incompressible Euler equation has been extensibly dealt with, such as local well-posedness
[
20
,
17
,
18
]
, ill-posedness
[
5
,
4
]
, global well-posedness issue
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
11
]
, blow-up criteria
[
2
,
9
]
, regularity issue of vortex patch
[
7
,
21
,
19
]
, and bifurcation of rotating vortex patch
[
6
,
15
,
14
]
.

In this paper, we focus on the solution with spiral roll-up structure. The flow with spiral roll-up draw a lot of attention due to its ubiquity in nature; flow near the wings of an airplane (Fig 81, 91 in
[
10
]
), flow after it pass through the solid corner (Fig 80, 95, 98 in
[
10
]
), boundary layer separation (Fig 32 in
[
10
]
), etc. Therefore, there has been a lot of attempts to understand spiral solution. For example, the 2D logarithmic spiral (
r\sim e^{k\theta}
) vortex sheet was introduced by Prandtl
[
24
]
in 1924 and Alexander
[
1
]
generalized it. The well-posedness of self-similar logarithmic spiral vortex sheet remains open for a century since Prandtl, but very recently, Cieślak et al.
[
8
]
gives reasonable sufficient condition for well-posedness of logarithmic spiral. The case of algebraic spiral (
r\sim\theta^{-\mu}
) has been studied also. Kaden
[
16
]
asserted in 1933 that in the initially straight shear layer, a vortex sheet with algebraic spiral (
r\sim\left(\frac{t}{\theta}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}
) structure emerges. Since then, several works had tried to describe this algebraic spiral more precisely, trying to find higher expansion term near the spiral center
[
26
,
22
]
. However, Moore
[
23
]
asserted that improvement of Kaden spiral could not be done by considering local behavior near the center, but could only be done by analyzing whole vortex sheet with Birkhoff’s integro-differential equation
[
3
]
. Although there has been a lot of effort to prove the existence of algebraic spiral vortex sheet rigorously, it is still an open problem until now.

In the mean time, a pioneering progress was made by a celebrated result in Elling
[
12
]
. Although the solution he constructed was differentiable solution of 2D Euler equation, not a vortex sheet, he proved rigorously the existence of Euler equation which shows some algebraic spiral structure (More precise definition of algebraic spiral structure is provided later). To state the main theorem of Elling
[
12
]
precisely, we first clarify some definition and concept.

1.2.
Some basic definition and related works

We use standard notations; for vector fields

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{1}\\
v^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad w=\begin{pmatrix}w^{1}\\
w^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}

and
2\times 2
matrices

A=(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2},\qquad B=(b_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2},

v\otimes w\coloneqq vw^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}v^{1}w^{1}&v^{1}w^{2}\\
v^{2}w^{1}&v^{2}w^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad\nabla_{x}v\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x_{1}}v^{1}&\partial_{x_{2}}v^{1}\\
\partial_{x_{1}}v^{2}&\partial_{x_{2}}v^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A:B\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}a_{ij}b_{ij}.

Definition 1.1
.

We say
u\in L^{\infty}\left([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)
is a weak solution of (
EE
) if

(1)

The velocity field
u
is weakly divergence free, i.e., for any (time-independent) test function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
t\geq 0
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla_{x}f\,dx=0.

(2)

For all divergence free test function
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty):\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(\cdot,0)\cdot h(\cdot,0)\,dx+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\cdot g_{t}+u\otimes u:\nabla_{x}h\,dxdt=0.

Definition 1.2
.

We say
w\in L^{\infty}\left([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)
2
2

2

We require
w(\cdot,t)
belongs to
L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
instead of
L^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
to guarantee
wu\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
is a weak solution of (
EE-v
) if

(1)

There exist
\psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);W^{1,1}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
u\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
such that
\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi=u,\,\Delta_{x}\psi=w
in distribution sense, i.e., for any test function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\psi(\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\cdot g)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\cdot g\,dx,\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{x}\psi\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}wf.

(2)

For any test function
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty))
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}w(\cdot,0)h(\cdot,0)\,dx+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}wh_{t}+w(u\cdot\nabla h)\,dxdt=0.

Remark 1.3
.

We will actually find (classically) differentiable
\psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{1}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
\Delta_{x}\psi=w
in distribution sense, i.e., for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
t\geq 0
,

-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla_{x}\psi(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla_{x}f\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\cdot,t)f\,dx,

and then define
u
by
\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi
. Then, it is trivial that
w,u
satisfies the first condition in Definition
1.2
.

Now, we define self-similarity. By scaling property of Euler equation, if
w(x,t),u(x,t),\psi(x,t)
is a solution for (
EE
) or (
EE-v
),

w_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a}}w\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right),\quad u_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-1}}u\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right),\quad\psi_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-2}}\psi\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right)

(1.1)

is also a solution of it for all
\lambda>0
.

Definition 1.4
.

A solution
(w,u,\psi)
of (
EE
) or (
EE-v
) is called
self-similar
if

w_{\lambda}(x,t)=w(x,t),\qquad u_{\lambda}(x,t)=u(x,t),\qquad\psi_{\lambda}(x,t)=\psi(x,t)

for all
\lambda>0
.

Lastly, for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
where
\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, we denote
n
-th Fourier coefficient of
f
by
\widehat{f}(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)e^{-inx}\,dx
.

Definition 1.5
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{A}^{s}(\mathbb{T})\coloneqq\left\{f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\,\Bigg|\,\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{s}(\mathbb{T})}\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left|\widehat{f}(n)\right|<\infty\right\},

where
\left\langle n\right\rangle\coloneqq(1+n^{2})^{1/2}
.

Now, we can state main theorem in Elling
[
12
]
.

Theorem 1.6

(Elling
[
12
]
)
.

Let
\mu\in(\frac{2}{3},\infty)
be given. Then, there exists sufficiently large
N_{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
g\in\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
satisfies
‘certain conditions’
, a weak solution of (
EE
) exists with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
. In particular, the solution is self-similar and any integral curve of
u-\mu z
is an algebraic spiral.

The
certain conditions
are

(1)

High periodicity:
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
for
N\geq N_{\mu}
.

(2)

Small
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
norm perturbation around constant: There exists a constant
c
such that

\left\|g(\theta)-c\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})}<\varepsilon_{N}\left|c\right|

for some
\varepsilon_{N}
determined by
N
.

Due to the embedding
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\mathbb{T})
, Theorem
1.6
requires that
g(\theta)
be close to a constant function in
L^{\infty}
-norm. However, as been mentioned in Section
1.1
, natural phenomenon have lead us to construct vortex sheet and this occurs when
g(\theta)
is a dirac delta distribution. Unfortunately, the second condition in Theorem
1.6
makes
g(\theta)
far away from dirac delta. Meanwhile, in 2016, Elling
[
13
]
took one step forward and develop his result in
[
12
]
. To state main result in
[
13
]
, we first clarify what it means by
spiral roll-up
in this paper.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
w(x,t)
be a weak solution of (
EE-v
) with initial vorticity

w(x,0)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta).

Define
A\coloneqq\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}\,|\,g(\theta)=0\right\}
. Then
w(x,t)
is said to show
spiral roll-up
if there exists a continuous function
h:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}\rightarrow[a,b]
, where
0<a<b<\infty
, such that

m\Bigg(\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\,|\,w(x,t)=0\right\}\Delta\bigcup_{\theta\in A}\bigg\{h(\beta,\theta)\underbrace{\left(\frac{t}{\beta}\right)^{\mu}(\cos(\beta+\theta),\sin(\beta+\theta))}_{\text{An algebraic spiral for all }\theta\in A,\,t>0}\,\bigg|\beta\in(0,\infty)\bigg\}\Bigg)=0

(1.2)

for all
t>0
. Here,
m(A\Delta B)=m(A-B)+m(B-A)
where
m
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If
g\in C^{0}(\mathbb{T})
, we require that

\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\,|\,w(x,t)=0\right\}=\bigcup_{\theta\in A}\bigg\{h(\beta,\theta)\left(\frac{t}{\beta}\right)^{\mu}(\cos(\beta+\theta),\sin(\beta+\theta))\,\bigg|\beta\in(0,\infty)\bigg\}

holds set theoretically.

Then, the result in Elling
[
13
]
could be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.8

(Elling
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\mu\in\left(\frac{2}{3},\infty\right)
and
\varepsilon>0
be given. Then, there exists sufficiently large
N_{\mu,\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
g\in\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
satisfies
‘certain conditions’
, a weak solution of (
EE
) exists with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
. In particular, the solution is self-similar and shows spiral roll-up in the sense of Definition
1.7
.
3
3

3

Elling
[
13
]
shows that
w(x,t)
is a weak solution of (
EE-v
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, not on whole
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The
certain conditions
are

(1)

High periodicity:
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
for
N\geq N_{\mu,\varepsilon}
.

(2)

Small
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
semi-norm compared to
\widehat{g}(0)
:

\varepsilon\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|<\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|

Compared to
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
embraces a larger class of initial data due to freedom in
\varepsilon
. Especially, as emphasized in the title,
[
13
]
admits initial data with mixed sign, e.g.,
g(\theta)=1+2\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
for some large
N
. However, due to the embedding
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\mathbb{T})
,
g(\theta)
should be a continuous function even though assumption of
L^{\infty}
-small perturbation from constant in
[
12
]
is eliminated.

Very recently, Shao et al.
[
25
]
made an remarkable progress after
[
13
]
. The main result of
[
25
]
is:

Theorem 1.9

(Shao et al.
[
25
]
)
.

Let
\mu\in\left(\frac{1}{2},\infty\right),N\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\left\{1\right\}
is given. Then, there exists
\varepsilon>0
, which is independent of
N
, such that if
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
satisfies
‘certain conditions’
, a weak solution of (
EE
) exists with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
. In particular, the solution is self-similar.

The
certain conditions
are

(1)

Periodicity:
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)

(2)

Small
L^{1}
-seminorm compared to
\widehat{g}(0)
:

\left\|g(\theta)-\widehat{g}(0)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}<\varepsilon\sqrt{N}\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|.

Theorem
1.9
is awesome, because they break an assumption that
g(\theta)
is continuous. Also, they eliminated the assumption of high periodicity and relieve the condition on
\mu
from
\left(\frac{2}{3},\infty\right)
to
\left(\frac{1}{2},\infty\right)
, making our distance closer to the goal of constructing spiral vortex sheet.

Now, we state our main theorem, which is located in the middle of Elling
[
13
]
and Shao et al.
[
25
]
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\mu>\frac{2}{3},\,1\leq p<2\mu
be given.
4
4

4

As we will see later,
\mu>\frac{2}{3}
condition is required to show
u
is a weak solution of (
EE
). Also,
1\leq p<2\mu
condition is need to show
w(\cdot,t)\in L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
For all

N>(2\mu-1)\left(397+\frac{1090}{\mu}+\frac{1264}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{999}{\mu^{3}}+\frac{42}{\mu^{4}}\right),

(1.3)

there exists a constant
C=C(N)
such that for all
g\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
satisfying

(1)

Periodicity:
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
,

(2)

Small
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}
-seminorm relative to
\widehat{g}(0)
:
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|<C_{N}\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|
,

there exist

w\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\quad u\in\begin{cases}L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{\frac{2-\mu}{1-\mu}}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))&\left(\frac{2}{3}<\mu<1\right)\\
L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))&(\mu\geq 1)\\
\end{cases},\quad\psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\left\{0\right\}))

(1.4)

which satisfy

1

u
is a weak solution of (
EE
) with with initial velocity given by (
4.10
).

2

If
\mu>1
,
w
is a weak solution of (
EE-v
) with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
.

3

This solution is self-similar and shows spiral roll-up in the sense of (
1.7
).

We briefly explain some remarks on Theorem
1.10
compared to previous results by Elling
[
13
]
and Shao
[
25
]
.

(1)

As
g\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
,
g(\theta)
needs not be continuous. Also, as
1<2\times\frac{2}{3}
, the most general
p=1
case is included for all
\mu>\frac{2}{3}
.

(2)

In the Theorem
1.8
in
[
13
]
, although it is computable, how large
N
should be was not clarified. This is because the author used
\lesssim
5
5

5

A\lesssim B
means that there exists some universal constant
c>0
such that
A\leq cB
.
instead of
\leq
when he estimated operator norm. Unlike in
[
13
]
, we will bound operator norm via
\leq
rather than
\lesssim
to estimate exact lower bound of
N
which needs to guarantee the existence of weak solution, hoping that
N
could be chosen reasonably small. Unfortunately, lower bound of
N
we get is larger than expected, with the order of
10^{3}
.

(3)

Our result implies Elling
[
13
]
since we use
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}
-seminorm. As we have

\sum_{n\in N\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|\leq\left\langle N\right\rangle^{-0.5}\sum_{n\in N\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|,

taking
N
large enough substitute the role of
\varepsilon
.

(4)

Shao et al.
[
25
]
can cover larger class of data than ours. In our result,
g(\theta)\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, while in
[
25
]
,
g(\theta)
just needs to be in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
. Also,
[
25
]
hardly require that
g(\theta)
be periodic (just
N\neq 1
is enough) and allow
\mu
to be
\frac{1}{2}<\mu\leq\frac{2}{3}
also. However, a big difference between
[
25
]
and ours is in how to measure the norm of
g(\theta)-\widehat{g}(0)
. If
g(\theta)
is given as a trigonometric polynomial, it is more convenient to estimate
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}
-seminorm than
L^{1}
-norm. For example, consider
g(\theta)
of the following form

g(\theta)=\sum_{n\in(kN)\mathbb{Z}\,\cap\,[-M,M]}c_{n}e^{in\theta},

(1.5)

where
N
is chosen so that (
1.3
) is satisfied. Since (
1.5
) is a finite sum,
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
condition is always satisfied regardless of
M
. To verify whether we can construct Euler solution with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
, we have to check

\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|c_{n}\right|\leq C_{N}

(1.6)

to apply Theorem
1.10
. Meanwhile, when we try to apply Theorem
1.9
, we have to check

\bigg\|\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}c_{n}e^{in\theta}\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\sqrt{kN}\varepsilon.

(1.7)

Since

\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|c_{n}\right|\quad\leq\quad\left\langle kN\right\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5+\varepsilon}\left|c_{n}\right|

for arbitrary fixed
0<\varepsilon<0.5
, to verify (
1.6
), it suffices to check

\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5+\varepsilon}\left|c_{n}\right|\leq\left\langle kN\right\rangle^{\varepsilon}C_{N}.

(1.8)

Therefore, no mater how small implicit
C_{N}
and
\varepsilon
be, we can make right hand side of (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) bigger than (say) 1 by taking
k
sufficiently large. For such
k
, to meet (
1.8
), we can choose any sequence
\left\{c_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5+\varepsilon}\left|c_{n}\right|
is convergence whose limit is less than 1 (For example, just
\left|c_{n}\right|<\frac{1}{n}
is a sufficient condition for
\varepsilon=0.1
). Then, even if we make
M
arbitrarily large, we can guarantee (
1.8
) holds.

However, for general
c_{n}
, there is no clever way to control

\bigg\|\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}c_{n}e^{in\theta}\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}

except a coarse triangle inequality

\bigg\|\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}c_{n}e^{in\theta}\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 2\pi\sum_{n\in((kN)\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\})\,\cap\,[-M,M]}\left|c_{n}\right|,

which leaves the possibility of divergence as
M\rightarrow\infty
even for
\left|c_{n}\right|<\frac{1}{n}
.

(5)

We show that constructed solution (
1.4
) is a weak solution for (
EE-v
) with an additional assumption
\mu>1
, while
[
13
]
and
[
25
]
only showed
u
is a weak solution of (
EE
).

1.3.
Sketch of the proof and organization of the paper

In Section
2
, with a self-similarity assumption, we rewrite (
EE-v
) in special coordinate, which is called adapted coordinate by Elling
[
12
,
13
]
, as below:

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\underbrace{\begin{cases}w_{t}+\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla_{x}w=0,\\
w=\Delta_{x}\psi.\end{cases}}_{\text{ In }(x,t)-\text{coordinate}}

\displaystyle{\Longrightarrow}\underbrace{\begin{cases}-\widetilde{w}+(\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z)\cdot\nabla_{z}\widetilde{w}=0,\\
w=\Delta_{z}\psi.\end{cases}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{In }(z_{1},z_{2})-\text{coordinate, where }z=xt^{-\mu}\\
\text{(self-similarity assumption)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle{\Longrightarrow}\underbrace{\begin{cases}w(\beta,\phi)=(\psi_{\phi}(\beta,\phi)-\psi_{\beta}(\beta,\phi))^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\quad\text{for some }\Omega(\phi),\\
L(\psi(\beta,\phi),\Omega(\phi))=0,\quad\text{where $L$ is defiend as in }\eqref{def of L}.\end{cases}}_{\text{In }(\beta,\phi)-\text{coordinate defined by }\eqref{new coordinate}\text{ defined on Sec }\ref{subsec: Special change of coordinate: From spiral to line}}

\displaystyle{\Longrightarrow}\begin{cases}w(\beta,\phi)=(\psi_{\phi}(\beta,\phi)-\psi_{\beta}(\beta,\phi))^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\quad\text{for some }\Omega(\phi),\\
\overline{L}(\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi),\Omega(\phi))=0,\quad\text{where }\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)=\beta^{2\mu}L(\psi,\Omega)\text{ with }\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi)=\frac{1}{\beta^{1-2\mu}}\psi(\beta,\phi).\end{cases}

In Section
3
, We solve
\overline{L}(\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi),\Omega(\phi))=0
. More precisely, we show that for given
\Omega(\phi)
, there exists
\overline{\psi}=\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}
such that
\overline{L}(\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi),\Omega(\phi))=0
. As you already have noticed, this is a tempting situation where we can use implicit function theorem. To apply it, we have to answer for these three questions:

\bullet
Question 1:
What is the Banach space
X,Y
and
Z
that will be a domain and codomain for
\overline{L}:X\times Y\rightarrow Z?
In other words, where does
\overline{\psi},\Omega,\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)
lives?

\bullet
Question 2:
(When we denote a trivial solution of
\overline{L}
by
(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
) Is the map
\bar{L}

C^{1}
in some neighborhood of
(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
?

\bullet
Question 3:
Is the partial Fréchet derivative
\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
isomorphism?

After some review of basic notion in functional analysis in Section
3.1
, we give the answer to
Question 1
in Section
3.2
. Using some properties of differential operator established in Section
3.3
, we give the positive answer to
Question 2
in Section
3.4
. We settle
Question 3
in Section
3.5
. Then, applying implicit function theorem, we can find
\overline{\psi}=\overline{\psi}(\Omega)
so that
\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)=0
for suitable
\Omega
.

Section
2
and
3
are almost same as that of Elling
[
13
]
except that we described in more detail for careful readers. Therefore, if you already understood Elling
[
13
]
, you can omit Section
2
and
3
.

In Section
4
, we first reverse the coordinate change we did in Section
2
and construct physical solution
(w(x,t),u(x,t),\psi(x,t))
via
\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi),\Omega(\phi)
in Section
4.1
. As we will guarantee suitable regularity for
\overline{\psi}
via constructing space
X
in
Question 1
carefully, change of coordinate we’ve done can be justified (i.e., it is
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism). A big difference between Elling
[
13
]
and ours emerges at this timing. In
[
13
]
, even though
\Omega
originally lives in
Y=\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})
, he restrict the range of
\Omega
to
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
. He would do so, because
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{T})
consists of continuous function and he use this continuity crucially in his proof. However, we restrict the range of
\Omega
from
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})
to
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
for
p\in[1,2\mu)
, from which we can include a larger class of initial data. In Section
4.2
, we show the constructed
(w(x,t),u(x,t),\psi(x,t))
is a weak solution of (
EE
) and (
EE-v
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\times[a,\infty)
for
a>0
. Through the investigation of convergence of
(w(x,t),u(x,t),\psi(x,t))
as
t\rightarrow 0
and some uniform-in-time boundedness in Section
4.3
, we show that they are in fact weak solution on whole domain
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)
on Section
4.5
. After we establish the relation between
g(\theta)
in Theorem
1.10
and
\Omega(\phi)
, we prove our main theorem in Section
4.6
.

Acknowledgements.
The author thanks In-Jee Jeong for stimulating discussions and mentioning
[
12
]
to us. The author also acknowledges partial support by Samsung Science
and Technology Foundation (SSTF-BA2002-04).

2.
Self-similarity and coordinate change

2.1.
Euler equation under self-similarity

In this section, we assume all functions are smooth, so there are no obstacles in manipulating equations. We will see in Section
4.1
that this assumption is valid at least in distribution sense as they have some reasonable regularity. Consider a 2D Euler equation in the vorticity form, which is derived from (
EE-v
) with smoothness assumption.

\left\{\begin{aligned} &w_{t}+u\cdot\nabla_{x}w=0,\\
&u=\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi,\quad w=\Delta_{x}\psi.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1)

Scaling property (
1.1
) of Euler equation says that if
(w,u,\psi)
satisfy (
2.1
), then

w_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a}}w\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right),\quad u_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-1}}u\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right),\quad\psi_{\lambda}(x,t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lambda^{a-2}}\psi\left(\frac{x}{\lambda},\frac{t}{\lambda^{a}}\right)

(2.2)

are also solution for (
2.1
) for all
\lambda>0,a\in\mathbb{R}
. Under the self-similarity assumption (
1.4
), take
\lambda=t^{\mu}
and
a=\frac{1}{\mu}
, then we have

\displaystyle w(x,t)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}w(\overbrace{xt^{-\mu}}^{\eqqcolon~z},1)\eqqcolon t^{-1}\tilde{w}(z),

(2.3)

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=t^{\mu-1}u(xt^{-\mu},1)\eqqcolon t^{\mu-1}\tilde{u}(z),

\displaystyle\psi(x,t)

\displaystyle=t^{2\mu-1}\psi(xt^{-\mu},1)\eqqcolon t^{2\mu-1}\tilde{\psi}(z).

Substitute (
2.3
) to (
2.1
) and applying chain rule yields

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\widetilde{w}+(\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z)\cdot\nabla_{z}\widetilde{w}=0,\\
&\Delta_{z}\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{w}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.4)

2.2.
Special change of coordinate: From spiral to line

As we are modeling spiral structure, it is natural to try to solve (
2.4
) in polar coordinate. Instead of using standard polar coordinate
(z_{1},z_{2})=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)
, following Elling
[
13
]
, we use
log-polar
coordinate.

(a,\theta)\mapsto(z_{1},z_{2})=(e^{a}\cos\theta,e^{a}\sin\theta)

(2.5)

for
(a,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. Note that in log-polar coordinate, algebraic spiral
\lvert z\rvert=\theta^{-\mu}
corresponds to the graph of exponential function
\theta=e^{-\frac{a}{\mu}}
.

Figure 1.
Exponential function in log-polar coordinate corresponds to algebraic spiral in Cartesian coordinate.

However, we want more than that: We want to find another coordinate change
(\beta,\phi)\mapsto(a,\theta)=(a(\beta,\phi),\theta(a,\phi))
such that

\displaystyle\textit{line parallel to $\beta$ - axis in $(\beta,\phi)$ - coordinate corresponds to}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\textit{algebraic spiral in cartesian $(z_{1},z_{2})$ - coordinate.}

The following observation gives us a clue as to how to achieve this goal.

Consider stationary, radial, self-similar, trivial solution of (
2.1
)

w_{0}(x,t)=C\lvert x\rvert^{-\frac{1}{\mu}},\quad\psi_{0}(x,t)=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-2}\lvert x\rvert^{2-\frac{1}{\mu}}

(2.7)

for
\mu\in\left(\frac{2}{3},\infty\right)
, where corresponding solution in
(z_{1},z_{2})
- coordinate is

\widetilde{w}_{0}(z)=C\lvert z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{\mu}},\quad\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(z)=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-2}\lvert z\rvert^{2-\frac{1}{\mu}}.

(2.8)

Computing an integral curve of
\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z
in (
2.4
) for explicit function (
2.8
) yields the following interesting result.

Proposition 2.1
.

For
\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(z)=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-2}\lvert z\rvert^{2-\frac{1}{\mu}}
, integral curve of
\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}_{0}-\mu z
is an algebraic spiral.

Proof.

Via direct calculation,

\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}_{0}-\mu z=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\lvert z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}(-z_{2},z_{1})-\mu(z_{1},z_{2}).

Hence, an integral curve
(z_{1}(t),z_{2}(t))
satisfies the following ODEs

\left\{\begin{aligned} z_{1}(t)^{\prime}&=-C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\lvert z(t)\rvert^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}z_{2}(t)-\mu z_{1}(t),\\
z_{2}(t)^{\prime}&=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\lvert z(t)\rvert^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}z_{1}(t)-\mu z_{2}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

Using this equation, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} v_{z,rad}(t)&=\frac{z(t)}{\lvert z(t)\rvert}\cdot\left(z_{1}(t)^{\prime},z_{2}(t)^{\prime}\right)=-\mu\lvert z(t)\rvert,\\
v_{z,ang}(t)&=\frac{z(t)^{\perp}}{\lvert z(t)\rvert}\cdot\left(z_{1}(t)^{\prime},z_{2}(t)^{\prime}\right)=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\lvert z(t)\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{\mu}}.\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore,

\frac{d\lvert z\rvert}{d\theta}=\frac{d\lvert z\rvert/dt}{d\theta/dt}=\frac{v_{z,rad}}{v_{z,ang}/\lvert z\rvert}=\frac{\mu\lvert z\rvert}{C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\lvert z\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{\mu}}}=-\frac{\mu}{C}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\lvert z\rvert^{1+\frac{1}{\mu}}.

(2.9)

Solving ODE (
2.9
) gives

\lvert z(t)\rvert=\left(a\theta(t)+b\right)^{-\mu}

for some
a>0,b\in\mathbb{R}
, which is an algebraic spiral.
∎

Figure 2.
Strategy for setting new coordinate: Make algebraic spiral in Cartesian coordinate correspond to a line in new coordinate.

Proposition
2.1
provides a strategy for choosing a new coordinate.
Suppose we find a self-similar profile
\widetilde{\psi}(z)
which satisfies (
2.4
). Since we are about to apply implicit function theorem to find a solution,
\widetilde{\psi}(z)
will be close to
\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(z)
in some sense from which we can expect that integral curve of
\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z
has spiral-like structure by the virtue of Proposition
2.1
. Therefore, given
\widetilde{\psi}(z)
, we choose
(a,\theta)=(a(\beta,\phi),\theta(\beta,\phi))
so that when we pull back integral curve of
\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z
, it becomes a line parallel to
\beta
- axis (Figure
2
). Therefore, for fixed
\phi_{0}
, we hope that
\theta(\beta,\phi_{0})
is monotonically increasing as
\beta
increases to make spiral winding. For this aim, it is natural to set
\theta(\beta,\phi)
as

\theta(\beta,\phi)=\beta+\phi

for
(\beta,\phi)\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
. Before stating one of the key proposition, we introduce some notation. Let
T
be a coordinate transformation map from
(\beta,\phi)
to
(z_{1},z_{2})
coordinate, which is the composition of two maps in Figure
2
. Then, we denote pull back of
\widetilde{\psi}(z_{1},z_{2})
to
(\beta,\phi)
- coordinate simply by
\psi(\beta,\phi)
6
6

6

Although we use same function symbol as in physical coordinate
(x,t)
, it may not cause confusion since the number of variable is different. Physical coordinate contains time variable, while
(z_{1},z_{2})
coordinate does not. We will not use space-time coordinate until Section
4
.
, i.e.,

\widetilde{\psi}(T(\beta,\phi))\eqqcolon\psi(\beta,\phi),\quad\widetilde{w}(T(\beta,\phi))\eqqcolon w(\beta,\phi).

(2.10)

Also, we introduce new differential operator as it appears so many time

\partial_{\varphi}\coloneqq\partial_{\phi}-\partial_{\beta}.

(2.11)

Then, we can achieve (
2.6
) through the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2
.

Assume
\psi\in C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)
and
a_{\varphi}
never vanish. If we choose

a(\beta,\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(-\frac{\psi_{\beta}}{\mu}\right),\quad\theta(\beta,\phi)=\beta+\phi,

(2.12)

the the pull back of integral curve of
\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z
to
(\beta,\phi)
- coordinate is parallel to
\beta-axis
7
7

7

For
a(\beta,\phi)
to be well-defined as in (
2.12
),
\psi_{\beta}
should be strictly negative for all
(\beta,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}
. This will be verified in Section
3.4
.
.

Proof.

Note that coordinate change map
T
, which is a composition of two coordinate change map; from
(a,\theta)
to
(z_{1},z_{2})
and from
(\beta,\phi)
to
(a,\theta)
, can be written explicitly

T(\beta,\phi)=\left(e^{a(\beta,\phi)}\cos(\beta+\phi),e^{a(\beta,\phi)}\sin(\beta+\phi)\right)=\left(e^{a}\cos\theta,e^{a}\sin\theta\right).

(2.13)

We can also explicitly calculate

J_{T}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{\beta}e^{a}\cos\theta-e^{a}\sin\theta&a_{\phi}e^{a}\cos\theta-e^{a}\sin\theta\\
a_{\beta}e^{a}\sin\theta+e^{a}\cos\theta&a_{\phi}e^{a}\sin\theta+e^{a}\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},

(2.14)

\left|J_{T}\right|=-e^{2a}a_{\varphi},

(2.15)

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{z_{1}}\\
\partial_{z_{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{1}{e^{a}a_{\varphi}}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\phi}\sin\theta+\cos\theta&-a_{\beta}\sin\theta-\cos\theta\\
-a_{\phi}\cos\theta+\sin\theta&a_{\beta}\cos\theta-\sin\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix},

(2.16)

where
J_{T}
is the Jacobian matrix of
T
and
\left|J_{T}\right|
denotes its determinant. Then, the
\phi
- component of pull back of
\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z
to
(\beta,\theta)
- coordinate is

\displaystyle\underbrace{(0,1)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{extract}\\
\phi-\text{component}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(J_{T}\right)^{-1}}_{\text{pull back}}\cdot\underbrace{\Bigg(\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{gradient}\\
\text{perp}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{\nabla_{z}}\psi-\mu\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}e^{a}\cos\theta\\
e^{a}\sin\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{z}\Bigg)}_{\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\widetilde{\psi}-\mu z}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{jacobian}}{=}-\frac{\psi_{\beta}}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}-\frac{\mu}{a_{\varphi}}.

Observe that the last term is zero if and only if

a(\beta,\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(-\frac{\psi_{\beta}}{\mu}\right).

∎

2.3.
Equations in new coordinate

In this subsection, we rewrite equation (
2.4
) in
(\beta,\phi)
- coordinate where the relationship between each coordinate is given by (
2.13
).

We first deal with the first equation in (
2.4
) under the assumption that
a_{\varphi}
and
\psi_{\varphi}
never vanish, which will be justified later.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EEz}}{=}-\widetilde{w}(z)+(\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\widetilde{\psi}(z)-\mu z)\cdot\nabla_{z}\widetilde{w}(z)

(2.17)

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{w}(z)+\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{z_{1}}\\
\partial_{z_{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{\psi}(z)-\mu\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\right)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{z_{1}}\\
\partial_{z_{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{w}(z)

\displaystyle\text{(after pull back)}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{diff change}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}-w(\beta,\phi)+\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\psi(\beta,\phi)-\mu\begin{pmatrix}e^{a(\beta,\phi)}\cos(\beta+\phi)\\
e^{a(\beta,\phi)}\sin(\beta+\phi)\\
\end{pmatrix}\right)\cdot\left(\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}w(\beta,\phi)\right)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{jacobian}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}-w-\frac{1}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\left(\psi_{\beta}w_{\phi}-\psi_{\phi}w_{\beta}\right)-\frac{\mu}{a_{\varphi}}w_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\Bigg(\underbrace{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{By }\eqref{new coordinate}\\
e^{2a}=-\frac{\psi_{\beta}}{\mu},~a_{\varphi}=\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\end{subarray}}w-\psi_{\beta}w_{\phi}+\psi_{\phi}w_{\beta}-\underbrace{\mu e^{2a}}_{-\psi_{\beta}}w_{\varphi}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}w}{2\mu}-\psi_{\beta}w_{\phi}+\psi_{\phi}w_{\beta}+\psi_{\beta}w_{\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\Bigg(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}w}{2\mu}\underbrace{-\psi_{\beta}w_{\phi}+\psi_{\beta}w_{\beta}}_{-\psi_{\beta}w_{\varphi}}\underbrace{-\psi_{\beta}w_{\beta}+\psi_{\phi}w_{\beta}}_{\psi_{\varphi}w_{\beta}}+\psi_{\beta}w_{\varphi}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}w}{2\mu}+\psi_{\varphi}w_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\psi_{\varphi})^{1-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\left(\frac{1}{2\mu}(\psi_{\varphi})^{\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\psi_{\beta\varphi}w+(\psi_{\varphi})^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}w_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\psi_{\varphi})^{1-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{e^{2a}a_{\varphi}}\left((\psi_{\varphi})^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}w\right)_{\beta}.

As we assume

\psi_{\varphi}\neq 0,\quad a_{\varphi}\neq 0,

we derive from above equalities that there exists
\Omega(\phi)
, depending only on
\phi
, such that

w(\beta,\phi)=\left(\psi_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi).

(2.18)

Next, we deal with the second equation in (
2.4
); the vorticity-stream relation
\widetilde{w}=\Delta\widetilde{\psi}
. We utilize adjoint formula to facilitate the calculation. The adjoint formula says that for
C^{1}
- coordinate change map
T:(\beta,\phi)\mapsto(z_{1},z_{2})
and
\widetilde{v}(z)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\widetilde{f}(z)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\nabla_{z}\cdot\widetilde{v}=\widetilde{f}\iff\nabla_{(\beta,\phi)}\cdot\left(\left|J_{T}\right|J_{T}^{-1}\widetilde{v}(T(\beta,\phi))\right)=\left|J_{T}\right|\widetilde{f}(T(\beta,\phi)),

(2.19)

where the divergence would be understood as distribution sense.

By adjoint formula,
\widetilde{w}=\Delta_{z}\widetilde{\psi}=\nabla_{z}\cdot\left(\nabla_{z}\widetilde{\psi}\right)
in
(z_{1},z_{2})
- coordinate holds if and only if

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\cdot\left(\left|J_{T}\right|J_{T}^{-1}\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\psi\right)=\left|J_{T}\right|w.

(2.20)

The right-hand side of
(
2.20
)
is simply

\left|J_{T}\right|w\underset{\eqref{determinant}}{=}-e^{2a}a_{\varphi}w\underset{\eqref{new coordinate}}{=}\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\mu}w.

The left-hand side of (
2.20
) can also be calculated by (
2.14
) and (
2.15
);

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\cdot\left(\left|J_{T}\right|J_{T}^{-1}\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\cdot\Bigg(\underbrace{e^{a}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\phi}\sin\theta+\cos\theta&-a_{\phi}\cos\theta+\sin\theta\\
-a_{\beta}\sin\theta-\cos\theta&a_{\beta}\cos\theta-\sin\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{\left|J_{T}\right|J_{T}^{-1}}\underbrace{\frac{1}{e^{a}a_{\varphi}}\begin{pmatrix}(-a_{\phi}\sin\theta-\cos\theta)\psi_{\beta}+(a_{\beta}\sin\theta+\cos\theta)\psi_{\phi}\\
(a_{\phi}\cos\theta-\sin\theta)\psi_{\beta}+(-a_{\beta}\cos\theta+\sin\theta)\psi_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{\left(J_{T}^{-1}\right)^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\psi}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\beta}\\
\partial_{\phi}\\
\end{pmatrix}\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{a_{\varphi}}\begin{pmatrix}\left((a_{\phi})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\beta}+\left(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1\right)\psi_{\phi}\\
(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1)\psi_{\beta}+\left((a_{\beta})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\phi}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left((a_{\phi})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\beta}+\left(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1\right)\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\beta}+\left(\frac{(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1)\psi_{\beta}+\left((a_{\beta})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle{=}\left(\frac{\left((a_{\phi})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\beta}+\left(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1\right)\psi_{\phi}+(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1)\psi_{\beta}+\left((a_{\beta})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\left(\frac{\left((a_{\phi})^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\beta}+\left(-a_{\beta}a_{\phi}-1\right)\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left(a_{\phi}^{2}+1\right)(\psi_{\varphi}-\psi_{\phi})+(a_{\beta}a_{\phi}+1)\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\varphi}+\left(\frac{a_{\phi}a_{\varphi}\psi_{\beta}-a_{\beta}a_{\varphi}\psi_{\phi}}{-a_{\varphi}}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\left(a_{\phi}^{2}+1\right)\psi_{\varphi}}{a_{\varphi}}-a_{\phi}\psi_{\phi}\right)_{\varphi}-\left(a_{\phi}\psi_{\beta}-a_{\beta}\psi_{\phi}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle{=}-\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}-\left(a_{\phi}\psi_{\phi}-a_{\phi}\psi_{\varphi}-a_{\beta}\psi_{\phi}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}-\left(a_{\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-a_{\phi}\psi_{\varphi}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle{=}-\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}-\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\phi}.

Therefore, equation (
2.20
) is transformed to

\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\phi}+\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\mu}w=0.

(2.21)

Using (
2.18
), (
2.21
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\phi}+\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\mu}\left(\psi_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega

(2.22)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon L(\psi,\Omega)=0.

As we will see in Section
4
,
\Omega(\phi)
is closely related to
g(\theta)
in (main theorem). Since our goal is to find a solution
w(x,t)
for given
g(\theta)
in Theorem
1.10
, our primary goal is to find
\psi=\psi(\Omega)
for given suitable
\Omega
. After finding
\psi
associated with
\Omega
, defining
w
as in (
2.18
) make (
2.4
) be satisfied, and then we can find self-similar solution of (
2.1
) through (
2.3
).

2.4.
Rescaling to eliminate decay

As we implied, we achieve above primary goal through implicit function theorem. That means we should find at least one solution for the map
L
. Obviously, we expect that pull back of (
2.8
) to
(\beta,\phi)
- coordinate should be a trivial solution for map
L
. In this subsection, we explicitly calculate pull back of
\widetilde{\psi}_{0}
and related
\Omega_{0}
to find a trivial solution of
L
. After that, we normalize stream function
\psi
to make trivial solution a constant function.

Remember that a trivial solution in
(z_{1},z_{2})
- coordinate is given by

\widetilde{w}_{0}(z)=C\left|z\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}},\qquad\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(z)=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-2}\left|z\right|^{2-\frac{1}{\mu}}

for some constant
C
. Also, we adopted coordinate change map
T
given by (
2.13
) and
a(\beta,\phi),\theta(\beta,\phi)
is given so that (
2.12
) holds. Then,

\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)=\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(T(\beta,\phi))=C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-2}e^{\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)a}.

(2.23)

Differentiating (
2.23
) in
\beta
- coordinate and use (
2.12
), we have

C\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{-1}a_{\beta}\,e^{\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)a}\underset{\eqref{eq1}}{=}\psi_{\beta}\underset{\eqref{new coordinate}}{=}-\mu e^{2a}

and this leads to

-\frac{a_{\beta}}{\mu}e^{-\frac{a}{\mu}}=\frac{1}{C}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right).

Integrating with respect to
\beta
coordinate, we have

e^{-\frac{a}{\mu}}=\frac{1}{C}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\beta.

(2.24)

Note that integration constant should be chosen zero for
T
to be bijective. Take the power of
1-2\mu
both side in (
2.24
) and substitute the value of
e^{\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)a}
to (
2.23
) gives

\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)=\left(\frac{C\mu^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{2-\frac{1}{\mu}}\right)^{2\mu}\frac{1}{2\mu-1}\beta^{1-2\mu}.

(2.25)

Also, from the relation

\displaystyle\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\beta\underset{\eqref{eq2}}{=}Ce^{-\frac{a}{\mu}}\underset{\eqref{trivial solz}}{=}w(\beta,\phi)\underset{\eqref{w formula}}{=}\left((\psi_{0})_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega_{0}(\phi)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq3}}{=}\left(-C^{2\mu}\left(2-\frac{1}{2\mu}\right)^{-1-2\mu}(1-2\mu)\beta^{-2\mu}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{C}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)^{1+\frac{1}{2\mu}}(2\mu-1)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\beta\Omega_{0}(\phi)

we have

\Omega_{0}(\phi)=C\mu^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}.

(2.26)

Since
C
was arbitrary, it is reasonable to choose

so that coefficient of (

) and (
2.26
) be as simple as possible. Therefore, we choose

C=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right).

(2.27)

With this choice of
C
, we fix trivial solution of map
L
from now on:

\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)=\frac{1}{2\mu-1}\beta^{1-2\mu},\qquad\Omega_{0}(\phi)=2-\frac{1}{\mu}.

(2.28)

Note that
\psi_{0}(\beta,\psi)
is a positive polynomial function with

\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0}\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)=+\infty,\qquad\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)=0.

In most of the situation, trivial solution is desired to be simple as possible. Since
\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)
is constant times
\beta^{1-2\mu}
and other non-trivial solution
\psi(\beta,\phi)
will be close in some sense to
\psi_{0}(\beta,\phi)
(remember, we find non-trivial
\psi(\beta,\phi)
via implicit function theorem), we deal with function after dividing it with
\beta^{1-2\mu}
, i.e., we deal with
\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi)
where

\psi(\beta,\phi)=\beta^{1-2\mu}\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi).

(2.29)

Investigating the map
L
shows that we need to calculate

\displaystyle\psi_{\beta}

\displaystyle{=}(1-2\mu)\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\psi}+\beta^{1-2\mu}\overline{\psi}_{\beta}=\beta^{-2\mu}\left(\beta\overline{\psi}_{\beta}+(1-2\mu)\bar{\psi}\right),

(2.30)

\displaystyle\psi_{\varphi}

\displaystyle{=}\beta^{1-2\mu}\overline{\psi}_{\phi}-(1-2\mu)\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\psi}-\beta^{1-2\mu}\bar{\psi}_{\beta}=\beta^{-2\mu}\left(\beta\overline{\psi}_{\varphi}+(2\mu-1)\overline{\psi}\right),

(2.31)

\displaystyle\psi_{\phi}

\displaystyle{=}\beta^{1-2\mu}\overline{\psi}_{\phi}.

(2.32)

For simplicity and convenience, we define
differential bar operator

\overline{\partial}_{\beta},\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}
by

\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\coloneqq\beta\partial_{\beta}+(1-2\mu)id,

(2.33)

\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\beta\partial_{\varphi}+(2\mu-1)id.

(2.34)

Then, we can represent partial derivatives of
\psi
as partial bar derivative of
\bar{\psi}
as followings:

\displaystyle\psi_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi},

(2.35)

\displaystyle\psi_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi},

(2.36)

\displaystyle\psi_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\beta^{1-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\psi_{\beta\phi}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi},

(2.38)

\displaystyle\psi_{\beta\varphi}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu-1}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}.

(2.39)

Substitute (
2.35
)
\sim
(
2.39
) to each term in
L
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\mu}\left(\psi_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{-2\mu-1}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\mu}\cdot\beta\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega

(2.40)

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu}\cdot\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{2\mu}\Omega,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\beta^{-2\mu-1}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\beta^{1-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}-\beta^{-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{2\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)_{\phi}

(2.41)

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2\mu}\left(\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)_{\phi},

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}

(2.42)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\beta^{-2\mu-1}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\beta^{-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\beta^{1-2\mu}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\left(\beta^{1-2\mu}\cdot\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)\right)_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq6}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\beta^{-2\mu}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right).

Hence,
L(\psi,\Omega)
can be represented as

\displaystyle L(\psi,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\psi_{\beta}\psi_{\varphi}}{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\phi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\varphi}+\left(\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}\psi_{\phi}-\psi_{\beta\phi}\psi_{\varphi}}{2\psi_{\beta}}\right)_{\phi}+\frac{\psi_{\beta\varphi}}{2\mu}\left(\psi_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega

\displaystyle{=}\beta^{-2\mu}\cdot\Bigg(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\partial_{\phi}\left(\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)+\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{2\mu}\Omega\Bigg)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\beta^{-2\mu}\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega).

3.
Main theorem in new coordinate

Through suitable scaling, we’ve reduced the problem of finding solution
\psi=\psi(\Omega)
of
L(\psi,\Omega)
to find
\overline{\psi}=\overline{\psi}(\Omega)
of
\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)
where

\displaystyle\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)=\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)

(3.1)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\phi}\left(\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)+\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{2\mu}\Omega.

Once
\overline{\psi}
is found, the process of recovering
\psi(\beta,\phi)
and
w(\beta,\phi)
is straight-forward through the relation (
2.29
) and (
2.18
). As we want to solve functional equation

\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)=0

by using implicit function theorem around trivial solution

\overline{\psi}_{0}\underset{\eqref{psi and psi bar}}{=}\frac{\psi_{0}}{\beta^{1-2\mu}}\underset{\eqref{trivial sol new}}{=}\frac{1}{2\mu-1},\qquad\Omega_{0}\underset{\eqref{trivial sol new}}{=}2-\frac{1}{\mu},

(3.2)

we have to answer following three questions:

\bullet
Question 1:
What is the Banach space
X,Y
and
Z
that will be a domain and codomain for
\overline{L}:X\times Y\rightarrow Z?
In other words, where does
\overline{\psi},\Omega,\overline{L}(\overline{\psi},\Omega)
lives?

\bullet
Question 2:
Is the map
\bar{L}

C^{1}
in some neighborhood of
(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
?

\bullet
Question 3:
Is the partial Fréchet derivative
\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})\in L(X,Z)
8
8

8

L(X,Z)
denotes continuous map from
X
to
Z
.
isomorphism?

First of all, we compute
\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
.

Proposition 3.1
.

The Fréchet derivative
\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})
is given by

\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})=\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\Big(\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+\mu^{2}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}\right)\left(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+2\mu\right)+\left(2\mu-1\right)\left(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\right)\Big).

(3.3)

Proof.

With explicit expression (
3.2
) and the definition of bar derivative (
2.33
), (
2.34
), we can easily calculate

\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}=-1,\quad\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}=1,\quad\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\overline{\psi}_{0}=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}=0,\quad(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}=-2\mu

(3.4)

Then, combining the following elementary calculation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\psi}}\left(\frac{(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}}{2\mu}\Omega_{0}\right)(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{0}}{2\mu}\left(\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\,+(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{2\mu}\right)\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle{=}\frac{2\mu-1}{2\mu^{2}}\left(\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\psi}}\left(\partial_{\phi}\left(\frac{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)\right)(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}\Bigg((\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\overline{\psi}_{0}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\Big)\cdot\left(-\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{2(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\left((\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\partial_{\phi}+\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}-\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}-\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle{=}\mu\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\psi}}\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}{\left(\bar{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\psi}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}\right)\right)(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega)

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\Bigg(2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)\cdot\frac{-(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{\left((\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\cdot\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\cdot\left(\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}-2\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{(2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\left(1+\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{2\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\cdot\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\left(\frac{-\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}}\partial_{\phi}

\displaystyle{=}\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\Big(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+2\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}-2\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\Big),

we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}(\overline{\psi}_{0},\Omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\bigg(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+2\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}-2\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mu\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2\mu-1}{2\mu^{2}}\left(\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+1\right)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+\overline{\partial}_{\phi}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\Big(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+2\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+2\mu^{3}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}+\mu^{2}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi}\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+(2\mu-1)\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+(2\mu-1)\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\Big((\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}+\mu^{2}\partial_{\phi}\partial_{\phi})(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+2\mu)+(2\mu-1)(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+\overline{\partial}_{\varphi})\Big).

∎

3.1.
Wiener type function space on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}
and its continuity results

The map
\overline{L}
and the Fréchet partial derivative
\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\overline{\psi}}
looks horrible and it seems hard to answer
Question 2
and
Question 3
. We overcome this difficulty by decomposing Banach space
X,Y,Z
and various differential operator into each frequency. For example, suppose
M
and
N
are some Banach space with
f(\beta,\phi)\in M,\partial_{\phi}f(\beta,\phi)\in N
. Instead of constructing
M,N
and showing the continuity of
\partial_{\phi}:M\rightarrow N
directly, we do Fourier transform
f(\beta,\phi)
with respect to
\phi
variable, i.e.,

f(\beta,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi},

Note that
n
-th Fourier coefficient
f^{(n)}
is not a constant but a function of
\beta
. Then, at least formally, we can represent
\partial_{\phi}f
as

\partial_{\phi}f(\beta,\phi)=\sum inf^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi},

so operator
\partial_{\phi}
corresponds to multiplication operator
in
in each Fourier mode. Here, we represent a key strategy for solving the problem; we first construct
M^{(n)},N^{(n)}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
where we expect
n
-th Fourier coefficient
f^{(n)}(\beta)
lives, and establish the (uniform) continuity of multiplication operator
in:f^{(n)}(\beta)\mapsto inf^{(n)}(\beta)
. We then construct Banach space
M,N
via
M^{(n)},N^{(n)}
and establish continuity of
\partial_{\phi}
by combining the continuity of
in:f^{(n)}(\beta)\mapsto inf^{(n)}(\beta)
. In this subsection, we gives some definition and properties of some Banach space to make above argument more precise.

Let
\left\langle\beta\right\rangle\coloneqq(1+\beta^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. For
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
, define
k
-th Schwarz seminorm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
by

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\\
0\leq m\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\left\langle\beta\right\rangle^{2}}{\beta}\right)^{m}\left|\partial^{(m)}f(\beta)\right|.

Then, the Schwarz class on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is defined by

\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})\eqqcolon\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})\,|\,\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}<\infty\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Also, we define
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
by the dual of
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
. Now, we define Schwarz class and tempered distribution on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}
.

Definition 3.2
.

The
Schwarz class
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}
is defined by

\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})\coloneqq\left\{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi}\,\Bigg|\,\left\|\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{q,k}\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}<\infty\text{ for all }q\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Next, we define dual space above Schwarz class. We additionally impose a condition rather than just defining as a dual of Schwarz class.

Definition 3.3
.

A continuous linear functional
T
on
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
is called
tempered distribution
if there exists a sequence of tempered distribution
\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
such that

T\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{(n)}e^{in\phi}\right)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}T^{(n)}\left(g^{(n)}\right)

(3.5)

holds for all
g(\beta,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi}\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
with
\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
being
polynomially uniformly bounded
, i.e., there exists
q\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}
and a constant
C>0
such that

\left|T^{(n)}\left(g^{(n)}\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.6)

Remark 3.4
.

Summation in (
3.5
) is well-defined due to polynomially uniformly bounded condition (
3.6
) and definition
3.2
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of Banach space that is embedded in Banach space
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
. We say
X^{(n)}
9
9

9

We abbreviate
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
to
X^{(n)}
when it is clear that it denotes a sequence rather than
n
-th component of sequence.
is
\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}
-
uniformly embedded
in
X
if there exists
M>0
such that
\left\|id\right\|_{[X^{(n)},X]}<M\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}
10
10

10

\left\|T\right\|_{[A,B]}
represents the operator norm of
T:A\rightarrow B
.
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular if
X^{(n)}
is
\left\langle n\right\rangle^{0}
- uniformly embedded in
X
, we simply say
X^{(n)}
is
uniformly embedded
in
X
and denote it by

X^{(n)}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}X.

Remark 3.6
.

A natural and concrete way to construct a tempered distribution is to take a function
f^{(n)}
on each Banach space
X^{(n)}
which is
\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}
-uniformly embedded in
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
11
11

11

By regarding a function
f\in X
as tempered distribution for some Banach space
X
which consists of function defined on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(e.g.,
L^{p}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
), it means there exists
C>0,k\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
g\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
,
\left\langle f,g\right\rangle=\int_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f}g
with
\left|\left\langle f,g\right\rangle\right|\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{X}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}
.
, with
polynomial norm decreasing condition
:

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty\text{ for some }q\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.7)

Then, for
g(\beta,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi}\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
,

\left\langle\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},g\right\rangle\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle f^{(n)},g^{(n)}\right\rangle.

Note that
\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies polynomially uniformly bounded condition since for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle f^{(n)},g^{(n)}\right\rangle\right|\leq C\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}-\text{uniform}\\
\text{embeding}\end{subarray}}{\leq}CM\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}

\displaystyle=CM\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s-q}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}\leq\underbrace{CM\left(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}\right)}_{\text{A constant}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s-q}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}.

Let
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the set of all sequence whose element is in
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
respectively. Then, how to define Fourier transform on
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
is obvious from their definition.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
f(\beta,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi}\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
and
T=\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})
. Then, Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is defined by

\mathcal{F}(f)(n)=f^{(n)}(\beta),\qquad\mathcal{F}(T)(n)=T^{(n)}.

For convenience, when we write
f^{(n)}(\beta)
for some function
f(\beta,\phi)
, it always means
n
-th Fourier coefficient of
f(\beta,\phi)
.

It is trivial from Definition
3.7
that
\mathcal{F}
is injective. When we try to define inverse Fourier transform, some care is needed due to the norm conditions in Definition
3.2
and
3.3
. Therefore, one can only define inverse transform
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
not on whole
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}}
or
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, but on
\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\right),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\right)
respectively.

Now, we are ready to define an important space which is main space of this paper.

Definition 3.8

(
\mathcal{A}^{s}
-space)
.

Let
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of Banach space that is
\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}
-uniformly embedded in
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
for some
q\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, the space
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)\coloneqq\Bigg\{T=\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T})\,\Bigg|\,T^{(n)}\in X^{(n)}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ with }

\displaystyle\left\|T\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)}\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|T^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty\Bigg\}.

Since
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)
is always associated with a sequence of Banach space not with single Banach space, we simply write
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
instead of
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)
without confusion.

Remark 3.9
.

Polynomially uniformly bounded condition (
3.6
) for tempered distribution is automatically satisfied since

\left\langle T^{(n)},g^{(n)}\right\rangle\leq\left\|T^{(n)}\right\|_{X}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}\leq M\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}\left\|T^{(n)}\right\|_{x^{(n)}}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}=\underbrace{M\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|T^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}}_{\text{uniformly bounded w.r.t }n}\cdot\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q-s}\left\|g^{(n)}\right\|_{\mathcal{S},k}

The first thing we should do is to show
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
is really a Banach space. We need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
G:X\rightarrow Y
be an injective linear map from a Banach space
X
to a vector space
Y
. Then, GX, the image of X by G, is a Banach space with induced norm

\left\|Gx\right\|_{GX}\coloneqq\left\|x\right\|_{X}.

With this induced norm,
G:X\rightarrow GX
becomes isometric isomorphism.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence Banach space which is
\left\langle n\right\rangle^{q}
-uniformly imbedded in
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
for some
q\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)
is a Banach space for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
equipped with
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
-norm given in Definition
3.8
.

Proof.

Note that
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a vector space with component-wise addition and scalar multiplication. Then,

\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)\coloneqq\left\{\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\Bigg|\,\left\|\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)}\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty\right\}

is a Banach space with the same proof as Riesz-Fischer theorem.

Consider a component-wise multiplication operator
M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)\right)
defined by

M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}\left(\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)\coloneqq\left\{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

It is trivial that
M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}
is injective. To show range of
M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}
is contained in
\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)\right)
, it suffices to check polynomial norm decreasing condition (
3.7
) for all
\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)
. Since
\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)
we have
\sup_{n\in Z}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty
. Hence

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|f^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty,

so
\left\{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}f^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies norm-decreasing condition (
3.7
).

Since

\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)=\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,\circ\,M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}\right)\left(\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)\right)

and
M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-s}}:\ell^{1}\left(\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)\right),\mathcal{F}^{-1}:\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right)
are injective, the result follows by Lemma
3.10
.
∎

Next, we investigate some continuity results regarding on
\mathcal{A}^{s}
-space.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
G^{(n)}:X^{(n)}\rightarrow Y^{(n)}
be a sequence of linear map where
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\left\{Y^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are sequences of normed vector spaces. We say
\left\{G^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
uniformly bounded
if there exists
M>0
such that

\left\|G^{(n)}\right\|_{[X^{(n)},Y^{(n)}]}\leq M

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
G^{(n)}:X^{(n)}\rightarrow Y^{(n)}
be a sequence of uniformly bounded linear map where
\left\{X^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\left\{Y^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are sequences of Banach space which satisfies the assumption in (
3.8
). Then,
\left\{G^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
induces bounded linear map

G:\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(Y^{(n)}\right)

defined by
G=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,\circ\,G^{(n)}\,\circ\,\mathcal{F}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Almost trivial. For
T=\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
, the map
G
can be written explicitly by
G\left(\left\{T^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)=\left\{G^{(n)}\left(T^{(n)}\right)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and through uniformly bounded condition on
G^{(n)}
,

\left\|G(T)\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(Y^{(n)}\right)}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|G^{(n)}\left(T^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{Y^{(n)}}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}M\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|T^{(n)}\right\|_{X^{(n)}}=M\left\|T\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)}.

∎

Remark 3.14
.

Although we’ve defined
\mathcal{S}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}\right),\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
in rather abstract and formal way, what we have in mind is very concrete as in the introduction paragraph of Section
3.1
. The specific element we intend to be in
\mathcal{A}^{s}\left(X^{(n)}\right)
is the function of the form

f(\beta,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(n)}(\beta)e^{in\phi}

which satisfies
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{s}\left\|f^{(n)}(\beta)\right\|_{X^{(n)}}<\infty
, where
X^{(n)}
are some (intricate) Banach space which is embedded in a set of continuous bounded function on
[0,\infty)
. A function
f(\beta,\phi)
can be regarded as tempered distribution by defining
