3&(\mu\geq 1)\end{cases}.

Then, we have
p>2
and
u(x,t)\in L^{2p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with its local norm uniformly controlled independent of t, because for
\frac{2}{3}<\mu<1
,

\left|u(x,t)\right|^{2p}\underset{\text{Cor }\ref{boundedness of three functions}}{\leq}A^{2+\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\mu}-1}}\left|x\right|^{2\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)-1}

with
2\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)-1>-2
. For
\mu\geq 1
,
u(x,t)
has no singularity at origin, so local integrability is trivial. When we denote conjugate exponent of
p
by
p^{\prime}
, then
p^{\prime}<2
and

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\underset{\eqref{eq66}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(B(0,2\delta))}

(4.32)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\cdot\left((2\delta)^{2}\pi\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq70}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\leq}(2M^{2}+1)(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\cdot\delta^{\frac{2}{p^{\prime}}}

for all
0<\delta<\text{min}\left\{\frac{R}{2},\frac{1}{M}\right\},t\geq 0
. We could get same bound for
\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f_{t}(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. Also,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\underset{\eqref{eq66}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\left\|\nabla^{2}(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(B(0,2\delta))}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla^{2}(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\cdot\left((2\delta)^{2}\pi\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq71}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\leq}(4M^{2}+1)(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\cdot\delta^{\frac{2}{p^{\prime}}-1}

for all
0<\delta<\text{min}\left\{\frac{R}{2},\frac{1}{M}\right\},t\geq 0
.

Now, all the preparations for proving Proposition
4.15
have been done. By Proposition
4.8
, using
(1-\zeta^{\delta})f\chi_{\{t\geq s\}}
as a test function, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,s)\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(x,s)\right)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[s,\infty)}u\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}\right)+(u\otimes u):\nabla\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f\right)\,dxdt=0

(4.34)

for all
0<\delta<\text{min}\left\{\frac{R}{2},\frac{1}{M}\right\},s>0
. Since
(1-\zeta^{\delta})f(x,t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash B(0,2\delta)\times[0,\infty)\right)
, all its derivative converges uniformly as
t\rightarrow 0
. Also, since we puncture the disk near origin through
1-\zeta^{\delta}
, Proposition
4.12
implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,0)\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(x,0)\right)\,dx

(4.35)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}u\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}\right)+(u\otimes u):\nabla\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f\right)\,dxdt

\displaystyle\underset{\text{Prop }\ref{initial data for vorticity prop}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\lim_{s\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,s)\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(x,s)\right)\,dx

\displaystyle+\lim_{s\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[s,\infty)}u\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}\right)+(u\otimes u):\nabla\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f\right)\,dxdt

\displaystyle=0

for all
0<\delta<\text{min}\left\{\frac{R}{2},\frac{1}{M}\right\}
. Now, we send
\delta
to zero. By (
4.32
) and (
4.33
), we have

\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,t)\right)\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{}\nabla^{\perp}f(\cdot,t),\qquad\nabla\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,t)\right)\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{}\nabla\nabla^{\perp}f(\cdot,t)

(4.36)

in
L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for uniform rate with respect to
t
. As
u\in L^{2p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(hence
u\in L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
also) with its norm on compact set uniformly controlled independent of
t
as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
, Hölder inequality yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,0)\cdot\nabla^{\perp}f(x,0)\,dx

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}u\cdot\nabla^{\perp}f_{t}+(u\otimes u):\nabla\nabla^{\perp}f\,dxdt

\displaystyle{=}\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,0)\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(x,0)\right)\,dx

\displaystyle+\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}u\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}\right)+(u\otimes u):\nabla\nabla^{\perp}\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f\right)\,dxdt

\displaystyle{=}0.

Since
\nabla^{\perp}f=g
, this completes the proof.
∎

In a similar way, it can be shown that
w(x,t)
is a weak solution of Euler equation of vorticity form (
EE-v
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)
. A difference is that we have to require stronger condition on
\mu
to utilize Proposition
4.11
and
4.12
for eliminating singularity at origin.

Proposition 4.16
.

If
\mu>1
,

w_{t}+\nabla_{x}\cdot(wu)=0

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)
in the sense of distribution, i.e., for any test function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\right)
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(x,0)f(x,0)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}wf_{t}+w(u\cdot\nabla f)\,dxdt=0.

Proof.

Take the cut-off function
\zeta^{\delta}
same as in the proof of Proposition
4.15
and let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\right)
be given. As we cannot assume
f(0,t)\equiv 0
, we cannot expect bound (
4.28
). Instead, we could just guarantee that there exists
M_{2}>0
such that

\left\|f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\left\|\nabla f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\left\|f_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\left\|\nabla f_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq M_{2}

(4.37)

for all
t\geq 0
. Let
M=\textnormal{max}\left\{M_{1},M_{2}\right\}
. Then, for all
0<\delta<\frac{1}{M}
,
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(4.38)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq66}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\left\|\nabla\zeta^{\delta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}+\left\|\zeta^{\delta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\left\|\nabla f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq67},\eqref{eq76}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\leq}\frac{M^{2}}{\delta}+M

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M^{2}+1}{\delta}.

Then, for all
r\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\underset{\eqref{eq66}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\left\|\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(B(0,2\delta))}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\left(\pi(2\delta)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq M(4\pi)^{\frac{2}{r^{\prime}}}\delta^{\frac{2}{r^{\prime}}}

with same bound for
f_{t}
. Also,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\underset{\eqref{eq66}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(B(0,2\delta))}

(4.40)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2\delta))}\left(\pi(2\delta)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq77}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\leq}M(4\pi)^{\frac{2}{r^{\prime}}}\delta^{\frac{2}{r^{\prime}}-1}.

Hence, if
r>2
, we have

\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\left\|\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=0,\qquad\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\left\|\zeta^{\delta}f_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=0,\qquad\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\left\|\nabla(\zeta^{\delta}f(\cdot,t))\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=0.

(4.41)

Now, we investigate the integrability of
w
and
wu
. As we saw in the Proposition
4.14
, we have

w(x,t)\in L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad u(x,t)\in L^{q}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})

(4.42)

for all

1\leq p<2\mu\qquad\text{and}\qquad\begin{cases}q<\frac{2\mu}{1-\mu}&(\frac{2}{3}<\mu<1)\\
q=\infty&(\mu\geq 1)\\
\end{cases},

where its norm on compact set is controlled independent of
t
. Hence,

wu\in L^{r}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})

(4.43)

for

r<\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{2\mu}+\frac{1-\mu}{2\mu}\right)^{-1}=\frac{2\mu}{2-\mu}&(\frac{2}{3}<\mu<1)\\
2\mu&(\mu\geq 1)\end{cases}

with its local
L^{r}
-norm uniformly controlled over time. If
\mu\leq 1
, we have
r<\frac{2\mu}{2-\mu}<2
, by which we cannot guarantee the convergence in (
4.41
). This is why we require
\mu>1
in the assumption of Proposition
4.16
. Then, if we set
r=1+\mu
, (
4.41
) and (
4.43
) are satisfied for all
\mu>1
.

We are ready to show that
w(x,t)
is a weak solution of (
2.4
). By Proposition
4.6
, taking a test function
(1-\zeta^{\delta})f\chi_{\{t\geq s\}}
gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\cdot,s)\cdot\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,s)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[s,\infty)}w\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}+u\cdot\nabla\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f)\right)\right)=0

(4.44)

for all
s>0
and
0<\delta<\frac{1}{M}
. By Proposition
4.12
,
w(\cdot,s)\rightarrow w(\cdot,0)
in
L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Since
(1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,s)
converges uniformly as
s\searrow 0
as a test function, we have

\lim_{s\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\cdot,s)\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,s)\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\cdot,0)\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,0)\right).

Note that by the assumption of
\mu>1
, Corollary
4.14
implies
u\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
w\in L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with their local norm controlled independent of
t
. Therefore, we have

\lim_{s\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[s,\infty)}w\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}+u\cdot\nabla((1-\zeta^{\delta})f)\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}w\left((1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}+u\cdot\nabla((1-\zeta^{\delta})f)\right).

(4.45)

Sending
\delta
to zero to include origin, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\underbrace{w(\cdot,0)}_{\underset{\text{Prop }\eqref{w is L^p}}{\in}L^{r}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\quad\underbrace{(1-\zeta^{\delta})f(\cdot,0)}_{\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{\eqref{eq94}}f(\cdot,0)\text{ in }L^{r^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{\text{H\"{o}lder}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\cdot,0)f(\cdot,0)

(4.46)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}\underbrace{w}_{\in L^{r}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\underbrace{(1-\zeta^{\delta})f_{t}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{\eqref{eq94}}f_{t}\text{ in }L^{r^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\\
\text{for uniform rate over time}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{wu}_{\underset{\eqref{eq96}}{\in}L^{r}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\cdot\underbrace{\nabla((1-\zeta^{\delta})f)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{\eqref{eq94}}\nabla f\text{ in }L^{r^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\\
\text{for uniform rate over time}\end{subarray}}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\delta\searrow 0]{\text{H\"{o}lder}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}wf_{t}+wu\cdot\nabla f.

Therefore, sending
s
to 0 and
\delta
to 0 successively in (
4.44
) yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(x,0)f(x,0)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)}wf_{t}+w(u\cdot\nabla f)\,dxdt=0.

∎

To prove
(w,u,\psi)
is an weak solution for (
EE
) and (
EE-v
), it only remains to show that:

Proposition 4.17
.

\nabla_{x}\cdot u=0,\qquad\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi=u,\qquad\Delta_{x}\psi=w

(4.48)

in distribution sense.

Proof.

We already show in Proposition
4.6
that
\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi=u,\,\Delta_{x}\psi=w
in the sense of distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. Also, since
u
is defined by
\nabla_{x}^{\perp}\psi
, it is trivial that
u
is weakly divergence free on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. To include origin, by the same way in the proof of Proposition
4.15
and
4.16
, it is suffices to show

\psi,\nabla_{x}\psi\in L^{a}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad u,w\in L^{b}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

for
a>2,b>1
, which is clear by Corollary
4.14
with
\mu>\frac{2}{3}
.
∎

4.6.
Initial data in original coordinate and main theorem

In summary, we construct self-similar weak solution of Euler equation through following process

\Omega(\phi)\underset{\eqref{main thm in special coordinate}}{\Rightarrow}\overline{\psi}(\beta,\phi)=G(\Omega(\phi))\underset{\eqref{w formula}}{\Rightarrow}w(\beta,\phi)=\left(\psi_{\varphi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\underset{\eqref{pull back notation}}{\Rightarrow}\begin{cases}\widetilde{w}(z)\\
\widetilde{u}(z)\\
\widetilde{\psi}(z)\end{cases}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Def }\ref{self-similarity}\text{ for}\\
\lambda=t^{\mu},a=\mu^{-1}\end{subarray}}{\Rightarrow}\begin{cases}w(x,t)\\
u(x,t)\\
\psi(x,t)\end{cases},

(4.49)

and we shows that
w,u,\psi
indeed a weak solution for (
EE
), (
EE-v
) in Proposition
4.15
,
4.16
,
4.17
.
As we used implicit function theorem, we cannot choose
\Omega(\phi)
arbitrary in
Y_{0,N}
, but sufficiently close to trivial solution, i.e. in
B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon_{N}^{*}\right)
where
N
should be chosen depending on
\mu
as in (
3.87
). However, we want to impose restriction on initial data on physical variable
w_{0}(x)\coloneqq w(x,0)
directly rather than on
\Omega(\phi)
, because limitation on
\Omega(\phi)
become ambiguous after transformation process (
4.49
). For this aim, we prove local surjectivity of initial data map which corresponds
\Omega(\phi)
to (
4.12
).

Proposition 4.18
.

The map which corresponds to angular variety of initial data (
4.21
) in physical coordinate from
\Omega\in B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)

\Omega(\theta)\mapsto\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\theta)\eqqcolon h^{(\Omega)}(\theta)

(4.50)

is a local
C^{1}
diffeomorphism to
Y_{0,N}
at
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
. In particular, this implies that, when we denote
h^{(\Omega_{0})}(\theta)\equiv\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\left(2-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)
by
\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}
, there exists
\varepsilon^{**}_{N}>0
and neighborhood
U
of
\Omega_{0}
such that if
\left\|h(\theta)-\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}\right\|_{Y_{0,N}}<\varepsilon^{**}_{N}
, there exists unique
\Omega(\theta)\in U\subset B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)
so that

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\theta)=h(\theta).

Also, if
h(\theta)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
, corresponding
\Omega(\theta)
also belongs to
L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

First, we check that the map is well-defined, i.e.,

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\in Y_{0,N}=\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right).

Using same method as in Proposition
3.41
,
3.42
, and the fact that
\mathcal{A}^{0.5}\left(W^{(n)}_{+,N}\right)
is an algebra, we have

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\in\mathcal{A}^{0.5}\left(W^{(n)}_{+,N}\right).

(4.51)

As a restriction map

\textrm{Res}^{(n)}:W^{(n)}_{+,N}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}

defined by
\text{Res}^{(n)}\left(f^{(n)}(\beta)\right)=f^{(n)}(0)
is uniformly bounded (
\because\left|f(0)\right|\leq\left\|f(\beta)\right\|_{C_{b}}\leq\left\|f(\beta)\right\|_{W^{(n)}_{+,N}})
, the induced restriction

\text{Res}:\mathcal{A}^{0.5}\left(W^{(n)}_{+,N}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right)

defined by

\text{Res}\left(f(\beta,\phi)\right)=f(0,\phi)

(4.52)

is continuous. Then, by Proposition
3.15
, we have

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\in\mathcal{A}^{0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right)~~(\because\text{ restriction)}}\underbrace{\Omega(\theta)}_{\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right)}\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right).

This shows the map (
4.50
) is well defined map from
Y_{0,N}
to
Y_{0,N}
.

To show (
4.50
) is a local diffeomorphism at
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
, we utilize inverse function theorem. First, we show (
4.50
) is
C^{1}
in
B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)
. By chain rule, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\Omega}\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\right)

(4.53)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\cdot id

\displaystyle+\Omega(\theta)\cdot\left(\frac{d}{d\overline{\psi}}\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\circ\frac{\quad d\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{d\Omega}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\eqqcolon I_{1}(\Omega)}\cdot id

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\underbrace{\Omega(\theta)\cdot\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\frac{1}{\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\eqqcolon I_{2}(\Omega)}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\circ\frac{\quad\partial\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\partial\Omega}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\eqqcolon J_{1}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\Omega(\theta)\cdot\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}\right)^{2}}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\eqqcolon I_{3}(\Omega)}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\circ\frac{\quad\partial\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\partial\Omega}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{\eqqcolon J_{2}}

Note that

I_{1}:B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)\rightarrow Y_{0,N}

is the composition of three map

\text{Res}\circ\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\circ G,

where

\text{Res}:W_{+,N}\rightarrow Y_{0,N}

is the restriction map to
\beta=0
as defined in (
4.52
),

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}:B_{X_{0,N}}\left(\overline{\psi},\delta\right)\rightarrow W_{+,N}

is a map defined by

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\left(\overline{\psi}\right)=\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}},

and

G:B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)\rightarrow B_{X_{0,N}}\left(\overline{\psi}_{0},\delta\right)

is a map in Theorem
3.54
. Since these three maps are continuous,
I_{1}
is continuous.

Now we investigate
I_{2}
term. Note that
I_{2}
is a product of two map;
\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
, and

\Omega\rightarrow\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\left(\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}\right)^{2}}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}.

(4.54)

Since (
4.54
) is continuous for the same reason as
I_{1}
, a map
I_{2}
is continuous as a product of continuous map.
I_{3}
is also continuous by same argument as
I_{2}
.

Since the map
\Omega\rightarrow\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}
is
C^{1}
on
B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon_{N}^{*}\right)
by Theorem
3.54
, and

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{\beta}

\displaystyle:X_{0,N}\rightarrow W_{+,N},

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle:X_{0,N}\rightarrow W_{+,N},

Res

\displaystyle:W_{+,N}\rightarrow Y_{0,N}

is continuous,
J_{1},J_{2}
is also continuous.
Combining these result on
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},J_{1},J_{2}
, we conclude the map (
4.50
) is
C^{1}
.

Next, we show

\frac{d}{d\Omega}\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\right)(\Omega_{0})

(4.55)

is an isomorphism. Let
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
in (
4.53
) and using the fact that

\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}_{0}\equiv 1,\qquad\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}_{0}\equiv 1,

we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\Omega}\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(\beta,\phi)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\phi)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}\right)(\Omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\cdot id-\left(1-\frac{1}{2\mu}\right)\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\left(\left(\overline{\partial}_{\beta}+\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\right)\circ\frac{\quad\partial\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\partial\Omega}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}

\displaystyle{=}\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\cdot id-\left(1-\frac{1}{2\mu}\right)\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}-1}\underbrace{\left(\left(\beta\partial_{\phi}\right)\circ\frac{\quad\partial\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}}{\partial\Omega}\right)\Bigg|_{(\beta,\phi)=(0,\theta)}}_{=0\text{ due to multiplication }\beta\text{ at }\beta=0}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2\mu}}\cdot id.

Hence, Fréchet derivative (
4.55
) is an isomorphism. As (
4.50
) is
C^{1}
in
B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)
and its Fréchet derivative at
\Omega_{0}
is an isomorphism, it is a local diffemorphism at
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
by inverse function theorem.

The last statement is clear since

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}

is bounded by two positive number as can be seen in (
4.1
).
∎

We gather all ingredients to prove main theorem.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
\mu>\frac{2}{3},\,1\leq p<2\mu
be given. For all

N>(2\mu-1)\left(397+\frac{1090}{\mu}+\frac{1264}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{999}{\mu^{3}}+\frac{42}{\mu^{4}}\right),

(4.56)

there exists a constant
C=C(N)
such that for all
g\in\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
satisfying

(1)

Periodicity:
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
,

(2)

Small
\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}
-seminorm relative to
\widehat{g}(0)
:
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|<C\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|
,

there exist

w\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\quad u\in\begin{cases}L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{\frac{2-\mu}{1-\mu}}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))&\left(\frac{2}{3}<\mu<1\right)\\
L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))&(\mu\geq 1)\\
\end{cases},\quad\psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\left\{0\right\}))

(4.57)

which satisfy

1

u
is a weak solution of (
EE
) with with initial velocity given by (
4.10
).

2

If
\mu>1
,
w
is a weak solution of (
EE-v
) with initial vorticity
w_{0}(x)=\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}g(\theta)
.

3

This solution is self-similar and shows spiral roll-up in the sense of (
1.7
).

Proof.

Suppose
g(\theta)=g\left(\theta+\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)
and define
g_{0}(\theta)
by

g_{0}(\theta)=\frac{\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}}{\widehat{g}(0)}g(\theta),

where
\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}
is as in Proposition (
4.18
) so that
\left|\widehat{g_{0}}(0)\right|=\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\|g_{0}(\theta)-\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}\right\|_{\mathcal{A}^{-0.5}\left(\mathbb{C}^{(n)}_{N}\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left|\widehat{g_{0}}(0)-\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}\right|}_{=0}+\sum_{n\in N\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g_{0}}(n)\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}}{\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|}\sum_{n\in N\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|.

By Proposition
4.18
, if
\frac{\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}}{\left|\widehat{g}(0)\right|}\sum_{n\in N\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\left\langle n\right\rangle^{-0.5}\left|\widehat{g}(n)\right|<\varepsilon^{**}_{N}
,
20
20

20

\varepsilon^{**}_{N}
is the constant in Proposition
4.18
.
there exists
\Omega\in B_{Y_{0,N}}\left(\Omega_{0},\varepsilon^{*}_{N}\right)
21
21

21

\varepsilon^{*}_{N}
is the constant in Theorem
3.54
.
such that

\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}_{\beta}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}{-\mu\overline{\partial}_{\varphi}\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}(0,\theta)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mu}}\Omega(\theta)=g_{0}(\theta)

for
\overline{\psi}^{(\Omega)}=G(\Omega)
(So the constant
C=C(N)
in the Theorem
4.19
is actually
\varepsilon^{**}_{N}/\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}
). Then, we can construct self-similar weak solution of Euler equation with initial vorticity
w(x,t)=g_{0}(\theta)\left|x\right|^{-\frac{1}{\mu}}
through
\Omega
by the process (
4.49
). We already saw that (
4.57
) follows from Proposition
4.14
,
1
follows from Proposition
4.15
,
2
follows from Proposition
4.16
, and
3
follows from Section
4.4
.

Although the angular variety of initial data was changed from
g(\theta)
to
g_{0}(\theta)
, we easily solve this issue by time scaling symmetry of Euler equation. We use the fact that if
w(x,t)
is a solution of Euler equation, so is
\lambda w(x,\lambda t)
for all
\lambda>0
. If
\widehat{g}(0)>0
,
\lambda=\widehat{g}(0)/\overset{\circ}{w_{0}}
gives the desired solution with initial data
g(0)
. If
\widehat{g}(0)<0
, it suffices to choose
C
in (
2.27
) as negative constant and repeat same process all above.
∎
